<meta>This page serves as the formal dedication to Christian August, Count Palatine of Sulzbach. It follows a series of biblical reflections on wisdom, transitioning from spiritual truth to the "perfection of medicine" through a personal anecdote involving the Prince and the famous chemist Van Helmont. The catchword "phura" at the bottom indicates the continuation of the Latin word "Sulphura" (Sulfurs) on the following page.</meta>

### To the Most Serene and Highborn Prince and Lord, Lord
## CHRISTIAN AUGUST,
### Count Palatine by Rhine, in Bavaria, Duke of Jülich, Cleves, and Berg, Count of Veldenz, Sponheim, the Mark, Ravensberg, and Moers, Lord of Ravenstein; My most gracious Prince and Lord.

#### Most Gracious Lord,

<image-desc>A large woodcut decorative initial 'E' featuring leafy scrolls and floral motifs.</image-desc>It remains fresh in my memory that Your Serene Highness, when I last traveled through those parts, discussed with me what the true and most direct way might be to reach the perfection of medicine <note>In this era, "the perfection of medicine" often referred to the discovery of a universal cure or the highest form of alchemical healing.</note>. To this, I [recalled] the golden teaching of the keen-sighted **Philosopher HELMONT** <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644) was a Flemish chemist and physician. He is a pivotal figure who combined traditional alchemy with early modern chemistry and is famous for his theories on the chemical nature of life.</note>: namely, they are the Sulfurs— <note>original: "Sunt Videlicet Sul- [phura]"</note>

—sulfurs <note>original Latin: "phura quædam," completing "Sulphura" from the previous page</note>, which, once they are corrected and perfected, the entire army of diseases obeys. This I presented to Your Serene Highness <note>original: "E. Durchl." (Eure Durchlaucht)</note>, and I not only proved the truth of this teaching regarding the **soul of antimony** <term>anima Antimonii: the essential, purified essence or "soul" of the mineral antimony, believed in alchemy to possess the highest medicinal potency</term> with strong arguments, but also—had my circumstances permitted a longer stay—I would have tangibly demonstrated it and brought it to light myself.

While what Helmont <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644)</note> praises here can be said with great truth regarding the **sulfur of antimony**, such a magnificent **eulogy** <term>encomium: a formal speech or piece of writing that highly praises someone or something</term> nevertheless belongs far more to the **tincture of copper**. This is because the tincture of copper has been more highly ennobled by Nature than that of antimony. Therefore, its **soul** <note>original: "anima"</note> is called the **Philosophers’ Sulfur** <note>original: "Sulphur Philosophorum"</note> by Helmont himself, as well as by **Paracelsus**, **Basilius**, **Valentinus** <note>A reference to the famous Swiss physician Paracelsus and the legendary (possibly pseudonymous) alchemist Basil Valentine</note>, and others. Its power [strengthens/relieves] the sick in their pains...

<summary>The author argues that while Van Helmont correctly identified the "soul of antimony" as a powerful medicine, the "tincture of copper" (Philosophers' Sulfur) is even more potent and naturally superior. He cites famous alchemical authorities like Paracelsus and Basil Valentine to support his claim.</summary>

# On the Secret
# Of the <term>Philosopher’s Sulfur<note>original: "SULPHURIS PHILOSOPHORUM." In the tradition of alchemy, sulfur was not just a chemical element, but a "principle" representing the soul, internal heat, and the active, combustible nature of a substance.</note></term>.
## The First Chapter.
### The Occasion for the Creation of this Little Book.

After the great mercy
of God ignited a particularly
bright light in medicine for us unworthy
humans in this <term>century<note>original: "Seculo"</note></term>—as a special comfort to the
sick—through the highly gifted <term>Jan Baptist van Helmont<note>1580–1644; a foundational Flemish chemist and physician who sought to reform medicine through chemical understanding.</note></term>,
this light has also been recognized and
held in great esteem by so many hundreds of physicians
who desire the truth; for this reason,
his profound writings left behind
have been read and contemplated very diligently. 
Nevertheless, it has unfortunately come to the point that
almost all complain about the obscurity
of the aforementioned books, especially

concerning the preparation of high medicines, and above all things regarding the <term>Tincture<note>In alchemy, a tincture is a potent liquid extract or essence believed to have transformative or curative powers.</note></term> or <term>Sulfur<note>original: "Sulphur." Not the chemical element alone, but the alchemical "soul" or active, combustible principle of a substance.</note></term> derived from <term>Copper<note>Associated with the planet Venus, copper was prized in "spagyric" (alchemical) medicine for its supposed ability to treat blood and nervous disorders.</note></term>. Since this has been judged by everyone—and quite rightly so—to be the most essential thing in medicine, it has happened that through extensive correspondence, one friend has sought the explanation of this secret from another. Everyone has been concerned to the highest degree about this golden <term>arcanum<note>original: "arcanum." A Latin term for a "secret" or a "mystery," specifically a hidden medical remedy or a divine secret of nature.</note></term>. I myself suffered for a long time from this same "sickness" <note>The author uses "sickness" metaphorically to describe his obsessive, restless desire for hidden knowledge.</note>; for although I had diligently read the instructive books of our <term>Philosopher<note>Referring to Jan Baptist van Helmont, whose work was established on the previous page as the foundation for this text.</note></term>, I could still form no clear concept from them of how to arrive at the unravelling of this secret. I therefore resolved to learn it by seeking out wise and sensible people. For this reason, I spared no effort in my travels, no matter how difficult, but sought out experienced people in this secret art here and there in various kingdoms—often at great risk to my life—and spoke with them about

In alchemy, this refers to the active, fiery principle within a substance that gives it color and medicinal power.</note></term> for me, or even provide the slightest information; indeed, even among the most world-famous, I found not a few who could not purely separate the <term>soul<note>original: "animam." The vital essence or most refined part of a mineral or metal.</note></term> from <term>Antimony<note>original: "Antimonio." This is the title of a work by Van Helmont, which translates to "On the Hunting for Sciences."</note> *On the Hunting for Sciences*, and I sought the truth of Nature no longer among men, but with the Creator and Origin of Nature through humble prayer. I very diligently read the writings of our Philosopher, paying close attention to his words and meaning, comparing and conferring one against the other, and then working industriously; thus, through the blessing of God, the truth finally appeared to me. ## The Second Chapter.
### Explains the Power through which a Mineral *Soul* or *Sulfur* must be *separated* from its Body.

If we wish to thoroughly understand by what means the <term>Soul<note>original: "Anima." In alchemy, the 'soul' refers to the internal, active essence or the "Sulfur" of a substance that gives it its properties.</note></term> must be separated from the hard, <term>coagulated<note>original: "coagulierten." This describes a substance that has been thickened or solidified from a liquid state into a solid body.</note></term> metallic bodies—such as copper, gold, iron, etc.—then we must first investigate how not only common sulfur, but also the <term>Sulfurs<note>original: "Sulphura." Plural form referring to the essential fiery principles within different mineral substances.</note></term> of the minerals (which are only semi-solidified bodies) are dissolved. By understanding this thoroughly, we may climb higher as if by steps and thus be advanced toward the separation of those much more firmly solidified Sulfurs. For because all metallic and mineral Sulfurs are of exactly the same nature throughout, and are called the <term>Element of Fire<note>original: "Element des Feuers." Alchemists categorized substances into the four classical elements; Sulfur was associated with fire because of its combustibility and active, "hot" properties.</note></term> by the <term>Philosophers<note>original: "Philosophis." This refers to the historical alchemists and natural philosophers who studied the secrets of nature.</note></term>, and are of a fiery nature, it must necessarily...

<summary>This chapter introduces the theoretical basis for separating the 'soul' or sulfur from metals. The author argues that one must first learn to dissolve softer minerals before attempting to work with hard metals like gold, as they all share a common 'fiery' nature.</summary>
<keywords>alchemical separation, Soul of minerals, metallic bodies, Sulfur of the Philosophers, Element of Fire, coagulation, mineral dissolution</keywords>

# Of the Secret of the
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

[who] has been sick, knows best how a sick person feels; and thus it is not unknown to me with what great longing the ignorant and those straying have sought and awaited this <term>Sulfur of the Philosophers<note>original: "Sulphur Philosophorum." This refers to the "Soul" or active solar principle of matter that alchemists sought to extract and refine.</note></term>. I have considered it a duty of Christian love to share with my <term>fellow brethren<note>original: "Mitbrüdern." Likely referring to both fellow Christians and fellow seekers of the alchemical art.</note></term> that which I have perceived—next to the grace of God—through much toil, time, and expense. I wish to set forth clearly to them the path I have walked and how I arrived at this <term>understanding<note>original: "Erkäntnis." More than just book-learning, this implies a deep, realized knowledge of natural truths.</note></term>, so that they may follow this small lamp I have set up. By its light, they may perceive this <term>secret<note>original: "Geheimniß." In an alchemical context, a "secret" is a hidden operation of nature that must be "unlocked" through practice and divine favor.</note></term> and also arrive at its <term>unfolding<note>original: "Auffwickelung." Literally "unrolling" or "unwinding," referring to the step-by-step revelation or practical completion of the alchemical process.</note></term>. Thus, they may do much good for their neighbor in his sickness, so that through many people, the great name of God may be praised for His wonders, and to Him, as the source of all good gifts, much praise may be given.

<summary>The author explains his motivation for publishing his findings, citing Christian charity and a desire to help others transition from ignorance to medicinal and spiritual knowledge. He frames his work as a "small lamp" to guide others through the complex process of understanding the Sulfur of the Philosophers.</summary>

<keywords>Sulfur of the Philosophers, Christian love, alchemy as medicine, spiritual knowledge, divine grace, neighborly charity</keywords>

# The Second Chapter.

## Explains the force by which a mineral <term>Soul<note>original: "Anima." In alchemical theory, this refers to the vital, animating essence or "Sulfur" trapped within a physical substance.</note></term> or Sulfur must be separated from its body.

If we wish to thoroughly understand by what means the soul must be separated from hard, <term>coagulated<note>original: "coagulirten." This refers to the process of a liquid or vapor becoming a solid, fixed mass.</note></term> metallic bodies—such as copper, gold, iron, etc.—we must first investigate how not only common sulfur, but also the <term>sulfurs<note>original: "Sulphura." The plural form of sulfur, used here to describe the internal combustible principles of various minerals.</note></term> of minerals (which are only half-coagulated bodies) are dissolved. By understanding this thoroughly, we may climb higher as if by steps, and thus be assisted in the separation of those much more firmly coagulated sulfurs. For because all metallic and mineral sulfurs are of entirely one and the same nature, and are called the "element of fire" by the <term>philosophers<note>original: "Philosophis." This term refers to the ancient and medieval alchemists who studied the secrets of nature.</note></term>, and are of a fiery nature, it must necessarily...

<summary>This chapter introduces the method for separating the "soul" or sulfur from metallic bodies by first studying how more easily dissolved minerals behave. The author posits that all metallic sulfurs share a "fiery nature" and must be understood through a hierarchical progression of complexity.</summary>
<keywords>sulfur, soul, anima, metallic bodies, coagulation, element of fire, alchemy, separation, natural philosophy</keywords>

follow/ that is, their dissolution must spring from a single foundation/ because, according to their origin, they are of one and the same essence and nature/ no different than how an old man and a newborn child are entirely of one essence, only that one is more perfect than the other; therefore/ if we wish to find the dissolution of the firmly <term>coagulated<note>As mentioned previously, this refers to a substance that has become solid or fixed.</note></term> Metallic Sulfurs/ we must first investigate on what basis and by what means the lesser <term>Sulfurs<note>In this context, the "lesser sulfurs" refer to common sulfur or more easily melted minerals, which are less "fixed" than noble metals like gold.</note></term> are opened up/ for in such a way have I tracked the truth, and thus have I found it. In alchemy, "fatty" or "oily" qualities were associated with the element of fire and the ability to act as a solvent for similar substances.</note></term> and fiery nature, dissolves sulfur as its own kind—for it is also a fiery being—and places before our eyes its otherwise hidden, blood-red <term>soul<note>original: "animam." ### Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers

just as any vegetable <term>Vegetable: In early chemistry, this refers to any plant-based matter or substances derived from plants</term> gives such a sour spirit <term>Spirit: A volatile liquid or vapor produced through distillation</term> when it is distilled; nor is the fiery, urine-like <note>original: "urinosisches"</note> salt, which is made with salt of tartar <term>Salt of Tartar: Potassium carbonate, a strong alkaline salt often used to release volatile components from other substances</term> or another alkali <term>Alkali: A substance that neutralizes acids; in this context, a mineral salt used to react with the salmiac</term>, that same spirit which the Philosophers <term>Philosophers: The alchemical masters who sought the underlying principles of nature</term> require for this work, since it is only the smallest part of the salmiac <term>Salmiac: Ammonium chloride, a salt prized by alchemists for its ability to carry other substances into a vaporous state</term>. The largest part, however, remains at the bottom, so that this salt is wrongly called a "spirit of salmiac," because the naming of a thing—when it consists of two parts, as salmiac does—always and primarily belongs to the largest part, as the saying goes: *the naming is determined by the greater part* <note>original Latin: "à potiori parte fit denominatio"</note>. Experience testifies to this: if one mixes a pound <term>Pound: A unit of weight; in the German pharmaceutical system, a pound was typically divided into 32 Loth</term> of salmiac with an alkali, then of the urine-like salt (which is truly no spirit, but only a volatile salt), only about 6 or 7 Loth <term>Loth: A small unit of weight; approximately half an ounce or 15 grams</term> rise up, while more than 24 Loth remain at the bottom with the alkali. To these 24 Loth, as the larger part, the name salmiac much more rightfully belongs than to the 7 Loth which [rise] up

### Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers

This **salt of urine** <note>original: "Sal urinæ"</note> always retains the nature of a **salt** and can never be made into a true **spirit**. Therefore, it carries up the **spirit of salt** <note>original: "spiritum salis"; hydrochloric acid</note>, the spirit of **vitriol** <note>original: "vitrioli"; sulfuric acid</note>, and other **corrosives** <note>original: "corrosiva"</note>—which are truly spirits—not in the form of a spirit, but according to its own nature, as a salt. # Of the Secret of the [Green Spirit]

...**volatile liquid of copper** <note>original: *cupri liquamen volatili*</note>, and in *The Duumvirate* <note>A work by the Flemish chemist and physician Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644).</note> he says that the **green spirit** <note>original: *spiritus viridis*</note> should be **remarkably volatile** <note>original: *insigniter volatilis*</note>; and this is solely so that the **body** <note>original: *corpus*</note> of the copper is properly prepared and made suitable to release its <term>tincture</term> <note>A "tincture" is the extracted medicinal or transformative essence of a substance, often concentrated into a liquid form.</note> afterward. For this reason, it must be so powerfully torn apart and reduced into the very smallest dust, as if into a subtle <term>spirit</term> <note>In alchemical terms, a "spirit" refers to a substance that has been refined into a volatile, vaporous, or highly active state.</note>. Otherwise, if this does not occur, its **soul** <note>original: *anima*; the internal, essential life-force or highest quality of a mineral.</note> cannot be obtained, as he says in express words in the place already cited from *On the Disease of the Stone* <note>original: *De Lithiasi*</note>, section 5:

> The **fire of Venus** <note>original: *ignis veneris*; "Venus" is the alchemical name for copper. ## Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers. 61

# Of the Secret of...

...[carried over from previous page] / but there, in the reversed **philosophical distillation** <term>Philosophische Destillation</term> <note>A term often used by alchemists to distinguish their "true" spiritual or chemical processes from the "vulgar" or common practices of chemistry.</note>, everything runs through the **filter** <term>filtrum</term>; yet it must be such a beautiful, singular **Philosophical green spirit** <term>spiritus</term>. Others have existed—and indeed the best among them all—who have remained with the actual literal meaning of "distilling" and sought it through the act of distillation itself. Because they could in no way produce such a green color from the **vitriol** <term>vitriol</term> <note>A group of chemicals consisting of hydrated sulfates, crucial for creating strong mineral acids.</note>, they surmised that the Philosophers must have added something else to the vitriol and kept it secret from us. 

For this reason, they drove the vitriol and the **saltpeter** <term>Salpeter</term> <note>Potassium nitrate, the other essential ingredient for creating nitric acid or "aqua fortis."</note> from two different **retorts** <term>retorten</term> <note>Glass vessels with long, downward-pointing necks used for distilling substances.</note> into a single **receiver** <term>recipienten</term> <note>The vessel at the end of a distillation apparatus that collects the condensed liquid.</note> using quick maneuvers. There, these two **spirits** <term>spiritus</term> <note>In alchemy, "spirits" are the volatile, liquid essences captured through distillation.</note> worked into one another, and through their action upon and against each other, a green **liquor** <term>liquor</term> or spirit was produced, at least in color. With this, they were satisfied; and indeed, this is still the best **method** <term>modus</term> among all of them—not for the sake of its assumed greenness, which is merely a reflection arising from the action of the two...

### Of the Sulphur of the Philosophers.

No force should be used, and one must pay diligent attention to the nature of things, how they wish to be driven <note>In alchemical practice, "driving" usually refers to the application of heat to move a substance or cause it to rise as vapor.</note>, so that one may do them justice and act according to Nature; such you must learn from experience itself and from the nature of the things you have at hand; for here all instruction ends that one person can give to another; there we must only go into the school of Nature and sharpen our understanding upon the subjects <term>subjecta</term> <note>original: *subjecta*; the physical materials or substances being acted upon in the experiment.</note> at hand; but above all things, we must pray for success from above, from our God and Father, in childlike simplicity and humility. Now, as was reported here regarding copper, the matter is the same with gold, which—after it is placed in its doubled and saturated *corrosive*—dissolves therein without any bubbling <term>ebullition</term> <note>original: *ebullition*; the state of boiling or the rushing of gas bubbles to the surface of a liquid.</note> like ice in warm water; and because this corrosive softens the gold even to its innermost parts, it also very readily passes over with it through multiple redistillations <term>cohobations</term> <note>original: *cohobationes*; the process of pouring a distilled liquid back onto its solid residue and distilling it again to concentrate its power.</note>, with very beautiful colors; for the tinting <note>original: *tingirende*; from "tincture," referring to the substance's power to transform and impart color to other metals.</note>

### Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers.

Therefore, let no one imagine that because the copper has been brought into such a **spiritual state** <note>original: *geistligkeit*; in alchemy, this refers to a substance being rendered volatile or vaporous through distillation.</note>—having been so dissolved and unlocked—it must always remain such a spiritual essence and cannot be separated from its added <term>corrosive</term> <note>original: *corrosiv*; a strong acidic solvent used to break down the physical structure of the metal.</note>. By no means! For although the entire host of common chemical writers—as their books clearly testify—unanimously teach (and greatly, yet ignorantly, rejoice in it) that when a metal is driven over by corrosive or acidic humors by means of multiple <term>cohobations</term> <note>original: *cohobation*; the laboratory procedure of repeatedly distilling a liquid off a solid and pouring it back over the residue to further refine or "extract" the spirit.</note>, they consider this to be the true **quintessence** <note>original: *quinta essentia*; the "fifth essence," representing the purest, most potent, and medicinal part of a substance, supposedly stripped of its earthly impurities.</note> of the metal or the **true drinkable metal** <note>original: *verum potabile metallum*; a liquid, medicinal form of a metal that was safe to ingest and believed to possess miraculous healing properties.</note>.

On account of this, they boast quite grandly, and thus until now one has misled the other, persuading listeners or readers to applaud through a sweet pretense. I could set down a great number of such <term>processes</term> <note>original: *processus*; the specific chemical steps or laboratory recipes.</note> from their books, were I not sparing of noble time and paper, and indeed

### Of the Mystery of the...
<note>This header is incomplete in the original, likely continuing a title from a previous section or leading into a larger chapter.</note>

...even our **Philosopher** <note>original: *Philosophus*; an alchemist or sage.</note> himself suffered from this "sickness" (during his early years of apprenticeship)—namely, ignorance—in that he believed that when he had brought over gold in the **form of a volatile, deep-red oil** <note>original: *forma punicei olii volatilis*; a common but deceptive result in gold alchemy where the metal appears to become a liquid oil.</note> by means of various <term>corrosives</term> <note>original: *corosiva*; strong acids or solvents used to break down metals.</note> and many repeated <term>cohobations</term> <note>original: *cohobation*; the alchemical process of repeatedly distilling a liquid off a solid and pouring it back over the residue to refine it.</note>, he possessed the true **drinkable gold** <note>original: *aurum potabile*; a legendary medicinal gold solution believed to be a universal cure and a key to longevity.</note>, and he rejoiced because of it.

But the matter is quite different, for there are two ways to dissolve metals: one is friendly and natural, while the other is violent. In the former, a thorough and **radical dissolution** <note>original: *radicalis solutio*; literally a "dissolution of the root," referring to a chemical change that fundamentally and permanently alters the substance's nature.</note> occurs, and the solvent and the dissolved substance cannot be separated from one another again. Instead, because of the great similarity of their two natures, the two become one and remain one—unchanging and inseparable. 

In the latter case, however—the violent dissolution—it is not so. For even if it appears to be a **radical dissolution**, the outcome nevertheless proves that there is a great **difference** <note>original: *differenz*</note> between this way and that, and...

Similarly, without such a proper means, this **Tincture** <note>original: *Tinctur*; the concentrated essence or "medicine" of the metal</note> would not be able to play <note>meaning to shine or manifest its properties</note> outwards. ### Of the Secret of ###

**Copper**, however, is a different matter; for copper and its **Sulfur** <term>Sulphur</term> <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the internal fire or "soul" of a metal, responsible for its color and combustibility.</note> are not **fixed** <term>fix</term> <note>A "fixed" substance is stable and does not evaporate or degrade when exposed to high heat.</note>. Instead, they exist in a middle state between entirely fixed **gold** and entirely **volatile** <term>flüchtigen</term> minerals. Because of this, its extracted **Soul** <note>original: **Anima**</note> could very easily be destroyed and consumed by the violence of the fire if one did not handle it with wisdom.

It is therefore necessary—according to the teaching of our **Philosopher** <note>Referring to Jan Baptist van Helmont.</note>—that this purely separated "golden" soul be **coagulated** and **fixed** <note>original: **coagulirt und figirt**; these terms refer to turning a liquid essence into a solid state that can withstand fire.</note>. This is achieved by the power of fire, which is capable of **ripening** <term>zeitigen</term> all imperfect things and making them constant.

Such fire, however, must be applied and used in the same manner as **Nature** herself does. Year after year, without ceasing, Nature ripens and "boils out" <note>The alchemical concept of natural "decoction," where the earth's internal heat matures ores over long periods.</note> unripe things until they reach their highest perfection. Nature performs this ripening in stages, beginning from a mild and weak degree of warmth and rising until the very highest degree of the grea- <note>The text breaks here; the catchword "sten" indicates the word continues as "greatest" (grössten) on the following page.</note>

However, the reader should not assume that by the term "metallic bodies" I mean only the well-known **metals** <note>original: *Metalla*</note> named by everyone—such as gold, lead, tin, iron, etc. Not at all. If that had been my intention, I would not have written "metallic bodies" in the title of this chapter, but would have used the term **metals** <note>original: *Metalla*</note> instead. Rather, by the term metallic **subjects** <note>original: *Subjecta*; the raw materials or base substances used in an alchemical operation.</note>, I refer to those **minerals** <note>original: *Mineralia*</note> that possess the same **fixity** <note>original: *Fixität*; "fixity" describes a substance's resistance to change or evaporation when exposed to fire.</note> as the metals. # Of the Philosophers' Sulfur #

What I taught in the previous chapter—namely, that the **Philosophers' Sulfur** <note>original: "sulphur Philosophorum"; in alchemy, "Sulfur" represents the active, fiery principle and the "soul" or "tincture" of a substance</note> can be obtained from other such metallic bodies that equal the metals in stability—might be more readily accepted and **conceded** <note>original: "concedirt"; granted or admitted</note> by the reader than what I now wish to prove: namely, that from completely **volatile** <note>substances that easily vaporize or change state when heated</note> minerals, such as **antimony**, **orpiment** <note>original: "Auripigmento"; a deep orange-yellow arsenic sulfide mineral used as a pigment and in alchemy</note>, **zinc**, and the like, just such a **treasure of health** can be **drawn out** <note>original: "educirt"; extracted or led forth from a raw state</note>.

Nevertheless, not only does experience itself testify to this, but many wise men also confirm it in their books, which I have undertaken to demonstrate in this **chapter**. And although it is not my primary intention to treat here of volatile minerals and the treasure of health they contain—as this little treatise is intended only to provide the **Philosophers' Sulfur** from **copper** in particular, according to the teaching and instruction of our **Philosopher** <note>likely referring to Paracelsus, mentioned on the previous page</note>, and at the same time...

<header>Of the Mystery of the</header>

To obtain this **jewel** and king among all **secrets** <note>original: *Arcanen*; refers to the *Arcana*, the powerful hidden remedies of Paracelsus</note>, he describes it thus: that one must first separate the combustible **sulfur** <term>Sulphur</term> <note>In alchemy, sulfur represents the "soul" or the principle of inflammability within a substance</note>, which gives off a blue flame when burning, from the **mercurial body** <note>original: *corpore mercuriali*; the metallic or fluidic essence of a substance</note>. This can be done in the manner I taught concerning **Antimony** <note>original: *Antimonio*; a metallic element used frequently in alchemical purification</note> in the second chapter; for as long as the minerals are volatile and yet combustible, the process for separating their **sulfur** from them is the same: namely, by means of a **fiery alkali**.

Upon such separated **sulfur**, one should pour its (appropriate and solely useful) **corrosive**—which is nothing other than the **Liquor Alkahest** <term>Alkahest</term> <note>a legendary universal solvent sought by alchemists, said to be able to reduce any body into its first matter</note>, as all circumstances sufficiently indicate—distill from it, and **cohobate** <note>original: *cohobiren*; the process of repeatedly pouring a distilled liquid back onto its solid residue and re-distilling it to increase its potency</note> it so often until the **sulfur** rises over the helm <note>the "helm" or *alembic* is the cap of the distilling vessel</note> as a red oil, etc. 

Because this can be practiced by no one except the possessor of the **Liquor Alkahest**, we shall merely pass by it and not delay ourselves there in vain; for whoever [possesses] such a **liquor**...

...well understand what these aforementioned words and names are meant to say and express. For this **Sun-glitter** <note>original German: *Sonnenglanz*; likely referring to the shiny, metallic luster of the mineral ore</note> is ground very fine and then mixed with a very fiery **alkali** <note>a caustic, basic substance used to dissolve the mineral</note>, and ground together in a **mortar** <note>original German: *Mörsel*</note> for half a quarter-hour <note>roughly seven and a half minutes</note>; afterwards, it is poured over with water so that it runs through the **filter** <note>original Latin: *filtrum*</note>. <page-num>175</page-num>

# Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers #

Among the **secrets of Paracelsus** <note>original Latin: *arcanis Paracelsi*; "Arcana" were hidden medicinal powers that Paracelsus believed could be extracted from nature</note>, our **Philosopher** places this **Sulfur of the Philosophers of Antimony** <term>Sulfur of the Philosophers: Not common brimstone, but the "soul" or internal heat/color of a mineral that carries its healing power</term> third in order, saying: **"In the third place is the tincture of the Antimonial Lily, which is of almost the same efficacy, etc."** <note>original Latin: *Tertio loco est tinctura Lili antimonialis, ejusdem penè efficaciæ &c.*</note> We must, therefore, go into the writings of **Paracelsus** <note>The influential Swiss-German physician and alchemist (1493–1541)</note> and investigate where he teaches how to prepare the **Secret of the Lily from Antimony** <note>original Latin: *Arcanum Lili ex Antimonio*; the "Lily" refers to a highly purified, often red, medicinal extract</note>; this we shall find in his ***Great Surgery Book*** <note>original German: *grossen Wundartzney*; Paracelsus's most famous medical work, published in 1536</note>, in the second book, third treatise, chapter 5, where he speaks of this **Sulfur of the Philosophers** in this way:

"The Philosophers have dealt much with **Antimony** <term>Antimony: A metallic mineral; Paracelsus was famous for championing its use in medicine despite its toxicity in raw form</term> and prepared it in many ways, but the right way has not yet been discovered by them. Instead, it is now brought to light, and it should rightly be an art that all physicians should know: For this purification is the entrance and beginning in all illnesses, whatever they may be; and if it were used, then so many people would not be ruined by those who only pretend to be physicians..."

<summary>This page discusses the third "secret" of Paracelsian medicine, the Tincture of the Lily made from Antimony, citing Paracelsus's "Great Surgery Book." It emphasizes that the correct purification of antimony is a fundamental starting point for treating all diseases.</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsus, Sulfur of the Philosophers, Antimony, Tincture of the Lily, Arcanum, Great Surgery Book, Iatrochemistry, purification of minerals</keywords>

<header>### Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers</header>

I shall set down a more certain **method** <note>original: *modum*</note> here, so that the reader who is eager for the truth can first carry out this one as a more certain and easier way, and afterwards can imitate that other one through the **reverberation** <term>reverberation: a process of heating a substance in a furnace where the flame is deflected or "reverberated" back onto the material</term>. Therefore, take the pure, separated **Sulfur of Antimony** <note>original Latin: *Sulphur Antimonii*</note>, as I taught how to make it in the second chapter; **dissolve** <note>original: *solvire*</note> it with the **volatile alkali** <term>alcali: a basic salt rendered into a gaseous or "volatile" state</term>, and **distill it back** <term>cohibire: an alchemical term for repeatedly pouring a distilled liquid back onto its solid remains to extract more essence</term> for so long until your salt rises up as a **ruby**, then you have a much shorter and more certain way. However, this **tincture** <note>original: *Tinctur*</note> is not quite as powerful as that made through the **reverberation**, because the latter has been more ripened and boiled out by the fire. Furthermore, if anyone is so inclined, he can also make an **essence** <note>original Latin: *essentiam*</note> out of the **Sulfur of Antimony** in exactly the same way as has just been mentioned regarding the **Sulfur of immature amber** <term>electri immaturi: "immature amber," which in this context refers to a specific mineral state of antimony or a related ore</term>; and that is then the true **Balsam of Sulfur** <note>original: *Balsamus Sulphuris*</note> for internal use, for there is no difference at all here, except that there will be a difference in potency.

### Chapter 12.
### That the **Sulfur of the Philosophers** can also be made from common sulfur.

Although our Philosopher <note>likely referring to the primary author being commented upon, such as Basilius Valentinus</note> does not teach how to prepare anything from **sulfur** <note>original: *Sulphure*</note> that equals the excellence of the **Sulfur of the Philosophers** <note>original: *Sulphuris Philosophorum*</note>, he is nevertheless not excluded from this. For although our Philosopher specifically names the **Sulfur of copper**, of **Glaura** <note>a term often used for mineral salts or the "dross" of metals</note> or **immature mineral amber**, and of **Antimony** by name in only one place, he nevertheless wishes to have common sulfur also included and understood among them, when he says in general:

> I therefore urge the students <note>original Latin: *tirones* — apprentices or novices</note> to learn how to strip the **Sulfurs of Minerals** of their foreign and poisonous power, under whose guard the vital fire is hidden, which most peacefully guides the **Archeus** <term>Archeus: in Paracelsian thought, the "inner alchemist" or vital force that governs the health and functions of a living body or substance</term> toward its desired goals. <note>original Latin: *Hortor itaqve tirones, addiscant Sulphura Mineralium spoliare vi peregrina ac virulenta, sub cujus custodia abditur ignis vitalis, Archeum in scopos desideratos placidissimè deducens.*</note>

That common sulfur is not excluded from this is also taught by the highly experienced **Paracelsus**, who counts it among those same **Arcana** <term>Arcana: the hidden, powerful healing essences or "mysteries" extracted from natural substances</term>...

### Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers. 185

Regarding the **sulfur of antimony** <note>original: *Sulphuris Antimonii*</note>, the **alkali** <term>Alkali: a basic salt, often derived from plant ashes, used here as a solvent to strip away outer layers of a mineral</term> is only rubbed with the **antimony**, water is poured on, and in the very same moment that the **alkali**—having become **impregnated** <note>original: *imprægnirt*; saturated or filled</note> with the **sulfur**—runs through the **filter** <note>original: *filtrum*</note>, the **acid** <note>original: *acidum*</note> is and can be added. Thus, the **alkali** is immediately separated from the **sulfur** again.

What kind of **ripening** <term>Ripening: the alchemical idea that substances "mature" or become more potent over time when exposed to heat or specific catalysts</term> or improvement can the **sulfur of antimony** receive from the **alkali** in such a brief moment? Yet, assuming that you could not **acquiesce** <note>original: *acquieſciren*; to agree or be satisfied with an explanation</note> in this, know that there are yet other ways to extract the **sulfur** so that no **alkali** touches it, nor any other thing works upon it, and yet it willingly and gladly rises out of the **antimony**. This occurs when one pours its own **vinegar** <note>original: *acetum*; in alchemy, this often refers to a sharp, distilled solvent</note> upon it. This **vinegar** wants to have absolutely no fellowship with the **sulfur**, but instead immediately joins itself to the **mercurial** <term>Mercurial: referring to the "Mercury of the Philosophers," the fluid, metallic principle within a mineral</term> part of the **antimony**. ### Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers <note>original: *Sulphuris Philosophorum*</note>

One should extract a **sulfur** <term>Sulfur: in alchemy, this refers to the "soul" or combustible principle of a mineral, not just the chemical element</term> from **antimony** <term>Antimony: a metallic mineral (stibnite) central to many alchemical works, believed to contain a powerful medicinal spirit</term> which, according to its external appearance, is not distinguished from common sulfur, except that it falls a bit more solidly <note>original: *gründlicher falle*; likely referring to its density or how it precipitates during extraction</note>. From such [sulfur], one should make a **cinnabar** <term>Cinnabar: a red mineral (mercury sulfide); here, a laboratory-created compound of the extracted antimonial sulfur and mercury</term> with **mercury** <term>Mercury: quicksilver, representing the "spirit" principle in alchemy</term>, and **sublime** <term>Sublime: to heat a solid until it turns into vapor and then collects again as a solid, a process used to purify and "exalt" a substance</term> the same seven times.

If a spoonful of this is enjoyed daily for several days in succession, it will perform such powers that one would have cause to marvel at it. Furthermore, such **philosophical cinnabar** can be used for this purpose without end or loss of its powers, provided that it is **sublimed** again.

### ->Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers<-

...bring [them forth]. Therefore, if you wish to bring the liquid, naturally simple, common sulfur to its perfection according to Nature, place it in a phial after it has first been **sublimated** <note>Sublimation is the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then collecting the purified solid as it cools and settles again.</note> a few times. Cook it by itself for as long, with such a fire as its nature requires, until it no longer rises, nor is combustible, but has become entirely **fixed** and perfect. This indeed requires a long time and good, steady supervision, so that it does not experience more heat than it needs; but on the other hand, the process is also plain and simple <note>original: *schlecht und einfältig*; in this period, these words meant "straightforward and uncomplicated" rather than "bad and simple-minded."</note> and demands no other preparation, costly **solvents** <term>Menstrua: From the Latin *menstruum*, these are specialized liquids or chemicals used in alchemy to dissolve or "digest" solid materials.</term>, or the like. It can happen purely and alone through simple fire in the **furnace** <term>Capelle: Referring to a cupel or the heated chamber of a furnace where refinement takes place.</term>. This is exactly how Nature works with metals within the earth, and in no other way. However, because this work lasts quite a long time and much time is required for it, so that you do not become weary during the work, nor remain constantly doubtful as to whether the sulfur is indeed preparing itself for perfection and...

<summary>The author describes a method for perfecting common sulfur through prolonged, steady heating in a phial, mimicking the natural process by which metals form in the earth. He emphasizes that this "simple" way requires only fire and patience, rather than the expensive chemical solvents favored by other alchemists.</summary>

### Of the Sulphur of the Philosophers

[way] <note>The text continues from the previous page: "If we now... reflect upon whether this previous [way] is the natural way..."</note> could not be improved or shortened, then the daily practice of cooks immediately comes to our aid and meets us; although they can, in a simple and plain manner according to Nature, ripen <note>original: *zeitigen*. All of which comes from the fact that the **mind** <term>Mind: original German *Gemüth*; in this context, it refers to the deep seat of the soul, including the will and emotions, rather than just the intellect.</term>, in which—as in His own **image** <note>original: *Ebenbilde*</note>—God intended to play with His outflowing wisdom and pour out the treasures of His eternity, is quite thickly darkened and overshadowed with earthly filth and mire; with **swinish desire** <note>original: *Säuischer Begierde*</note> for earthly, fleshly, and transitory things; with devilish pride, avarice, ambition, lust of the flesh, and with insatiable desire for earthly, transitory wealth, which is yet but a shadow that vanishes in one's hands.

# Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers. 21 #

...by itself quite dry / and thus is **dissolved** <term>solvirt; from the Latin *solvere*, meaning to break down or dissolve a substance into its components</term> with such a gentle fire in a sealed vessel, so that the **sulfur** <note>original: *Schwefel*</note> does not rise <note>i.e., does not sublime or escape as vapor</note>. You can now choose for yourself whichever way pleases you best; and know for your information that this latter way is indeed better than the first, but on the other hand also more laborious, while the former is much easier; therefore choose whichever is most convenient for you. ...the understanding relates to the things themselves. For the **understanding** <term>intellectus; the higher, intuitive faculty of the mind that grasps truth directly</term> knows things just as they are, and therefore, in turn, the understanding is validated <note>original Latin: *vericatur*</note> concerning the things themselves through the things themselves. Since the "being" of things is always true in itself, and their essence is truth itself, the understanding which is directed upon them is always directly true.

But since the **imagination**, or its **reasoning** <term>ratio; the logical, step-by-step faculty</term>, is an indirect <note>original: *obliquus*; literally "slanting" or "crooked," as opposed to the "direct" path of intuitive understanding</note> mode of understanding—proceeding through logic and discourse rather than through the transformation of **adequacy** <term>adequatio; the perfect correspondence or "matching" between the mind and the object it observes</term>—therefore, this rational mode is a deceptive and misleading way of understanding. 

However, the good, the upright, the one, and the true always maintain themselves in one and the same way within the understanding, because in the understanding they stand at the point of adequacy; but the evil, the crooked, the indirect, the false, and the manifold are produced in many ways through the **reasoning** <term>rationem</term> in the imaginative faculty. Therefore, I have certainly realized that Reason should not be held in such high regard as it has been until now, and even more, that in

# Of the Sulfur of the Philosophers #
<note>original: *Sulphuris Philosophorum*; in alchemy, "Sulfur" represents the active, solar, or spiritual principle within a substance.</note>

...[knowledge which] can be had in this life regarding created things; because in the light of its own light, it <note>the soul or intellect</note> in some way gazes upon the properties, essences, effects, differences, distinctions, and defects of all things. Therefore, once this **knowledge** <note>original: *cognitio*</note> has arrived at that point, all nobler speculation and assistance of **Reason** <term>Ratio; here meaning discursive, logical thought that moves from one premise to another, which the author considers inferior to direct spiritual intuition</term> withers away; just as, conversely, the true **understanding** <term>intellectus; the higher faculty of the mind capable of direct, non-logical apprehension of truth</term> is suppressed within us under the dictates of Reason. And in section 60, I have sufficiently shown elsewhere that in the whole of nature, the doctrine of **Aristotle** <note>The author is critiquing the traditional Scholastic reliance on Aristotelian logic, which dominated medieval universities.</note> is empty and mere trifles; how much less, then, would it hold up in the realm of the intellect? For the being and operation of the intellect depend upon the soul alone. For we Christians are compelled to believe that our intellect is an **immortal spirit**, the light and image of the Almighty. Just as its origin transcends nature, so it cannot be fitted to nature's rules, since it possesses a **simplest essence** <term>esse simplicissimum; a philosophical term for a being that is not made of parts and is therefore indivisible and indestructible</term>, and also because it depends immediately, totally, and continually upon its **original archetype** <note>original: *originali typo*; referring to God as the original model of which the human intellect is a copy</note>, so much so that without a particular grace it cannot

<meta>The catchword "qvic-" suggests the next page begins with "quicquid" (whatever) or a similar word, continuing the thought on the necessity of divine grace.</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the higher intellect provides a direct, spiritual insight into the essence of things that far surpasses Aristotelian logic and human reason. He defines the human intellect as an immortal spirit and a direct reflection of God, operating outside the standard laws of nature.</summary>

<page-num>242</page-num>

<header>Of the Mystery of the</header>
<note>original: *Vom Geheimnis des*; this heading remains incomplete as the text transitions from the German title into the Latin treatise.</note>

[...to] understand anything, because the object of the **intellect** <term>intellectus; the soul's faculty for direct spiritual perception, distinct from logical reasoning</term> is **truth** <note>original: *veritas*</note> itself. For this reason, it cannot understand with a perfect understanding except by receiving; and what it merely receives, it undergoes passively <note>original: *patitur*; in this philosophical context, "passion" or "suffering" refers to the state of being a recipient of action rather than the cause of it</note>, rather than acting upon it. For that which comes to it through **grace** <note>original: *gratiam*</note> is not inherent to the intellect itself; rather, the intellect undergoes this experience only by understanding through illumination. Indeed, it is more troublesome, servile, and obscure to work through active reasoning than to passively receive, because in receiving, the intellect takes in a nobler **light** <note>original: *lumen*</note> that is freely bestowed. 

Finally, since in the act of understanding it always transmigrates into the form of the thing understood, it thus participates in an **unbounded light** <note>original: *lumine interminato*</note>. It is perfected without the weariness, trouble, or labor of [ordinary] thinking; and the light shines upon the object through the act of understanding, while the intellect itself shines in that light. It is as if the things themselves seem to speak to us without words; the intellect penetrates them even when they are closed, just as if they had been cut open and laid bare—

<summary>This page describes the intellect as a passive recipient of divine truth, arguing that spiritual "light" allows for a direct, effortless understanding of the essence of objects that surpasses active logical effort.</summary>

<keywords>intellect, truth, divine grace, illumination, passive reception, spiritual light, essence of things</keywords>

<page-num>243</page-num>

# Of the Philosophers' Sulfur
<note>original: *Sulphuris Philosophorum*; in alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, solar, or spiritual principle within the soul and matter.</note>

[...clari]ty. Therefore, the **intellect** <term>intellectus; the soul's faculty for direct spiritual perception</term> is always perfected by undergoing <note>original: *patiendo*; to be acted upon by a higher force</note> and by receiving. And in section 63:

1. I believe, first and foremost, that nothing reaches the knowledge of **truth** <note>original: *veritatis*</note> except through faith and the intellect.
2. All truth emanates from a single truth, which is therefore the most primitive <note>in the sense of the "first" or "original" source</note>.
3. Every intellect derives from a single and infinite intellect.
4. Just as all **light** <note>original: *lumen*</note> comes from a single light.
5. Therefore, the essence of truth differs in no way from the essence of the intellect.
6. Our own intellect is empty, void, poor, and dark.
7. All its clarity, nobility, fullness, light, and truth come to it only through receiving and undergoing <note>being the recipient of divine action</note>.
8. It is ennobled all the more as it "suffers" <note>undergoes the influence</note> more from the light, which is above all nature.

If the eager **seeker of truth** <note>original: *Sucher der Wahrheit*</note> follows this instruction and puts it into practice, he will experience that there is no other way to recognize the truth of natural powers than through the **turning of the senses inward** <note>original: *einkehrung der Sinne hinneinwerts*</note> into the...

<summary>This page argues that the human intellect is a dark, empty vessel that only achieves truth and clarity by passively receiving divine light. It concludes with a practical call for the seeker to turn their senses inward to find these natural powers.</summary>

<keywords>Intellect, Divine Light, Truth, Sulfur of the Philosophers, Passivity, Inwardness, Alchemy of the Soul</keywords>

**center of the soul** <note>original: *centrum der Seelen*; in mystical theology, this refers to the "ground of the soul" or the innermost point where the human spirit meets the Divine.</note>, where God dwells. If God is called upon with childlike faith for wisdom and understanding, it is impossible for Him to withhold Himself; as the **highest Good**, He must share His gifts abundantly with His poor creature.
